A Meta Theorem for nonlinear stochastic coupled systems: Application to stochastic chemotaxis-Stokes porous media Thanks: Boris Jidjou Moghomye was supported by the Austrian Science Foundation (FWF), Project P 32295, Moreover we would like to thank Paul Razafamandimby, City University Dublin, Ireland, for his inspiring discussions and helpful remarks.
Erika Hausenblas, Boris Jidjou Moghomye

Abstract
. The purpose of the paper is twofold. Firstly, we want to present a Meta Theorem to show the existence of a martingale solution for coupled systems of non-linear stochastic differential equations. The idea is first to split the system by rewriting the non-linear part in a linear part acting on a given process ξ \xi . This is done in such a way that the fixpoint with respect to ξ \xi would be the solution. However, to show the well posedness of the linearized system, one needs a cut-off argument. Under which conditions one can handle the limits of the cut-off parameter to get in the end a martingale solution of the original system is given in the Meta-Theorem. This approached was used in [ 14 ] to show the existence of a martingale solution of a chemical activator-inhibitor system. Secondly, we want to verify the full applicability of the Meta Theorem by showing the existence of a martingale solution of a highly nonlinear chemotaxis system with underlying fluid dynamic. In particular, we investigate the coupled system { d n + 𝐮 ⋅ ∇ n d t = [ r n Δ | n | q − 1 n − χ div ( n ∇ c ) ] d t + g γ 1 ( n ) ∘ d W 1 ( t ) , d c + 𝐮 ⋅ ∇ c d t = [ r c Δ c − ζ c + β n ] d t + g γ 2 ( c ) ∘ d W 2 ( t ) , d 𝐮 + ∇ P d t = [ r 𝐮 Δ 𝐮 + n ⋆ Φ ] d t + σ γ 3 d W 3 ( t ) , ∇ ⋅ 𝐮 = 0 , \displaystyle\begin{cases}d{n}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla ndt=\left[r_{n}\Delta|n|^{q-1}n-\chi\mbox{div}(n\nabla c)\right]\,dt+g_{\gamma_{1}}(n)\circ dW_{1}(t),\\ d{c}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla cdt=\left[r_{c}\Delta c-\zeta c+\beta n\right]\,dt+g_{\gamma_{2}}(c)\circ dW_{2}(t),\\ d\mathbf{u}+\nabla Pdt=\left[r_{\mathbf{u}}\Delta\mathbf{u}+n\star\Phi\right]dt+\sigma_{\gamma_{3}}\,dW_{3}(t),\\ \nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}=0,\end{cases} with initial condition ( n 0 , c 0 , 𝐮 0 ) (n_{0},c_{0},\mathbf{u}_{0}) on a filtered probability space 𝔄 \mathfrak{A} and W 1 W_{1} , W 2 W_{2} , and W 3 W_{3} be three independent time homogeneous spatial Wiener processes over 𝔄 \mathfrak{A} . Here n n is the cell density, c c is the concentration of the chemical signal, and 𝐮 \mathbf{u} is a vector field over 𝒪 \mathcal{O} . The positive terms r n r_{n} and r c r_{c} are the diffusivity of the cells and chemoattractant, respectively, the positive value χ \chi is the chemotactic sensitivity.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 35K57, 60H15; Secondary 37N25, 47H10, 76S05, 92C15

1.
Introduction

Cross-diffusion systems are a type of coupled partial differential equations system that arises in many applications involving multi-species transport. In these systems, the diffusion coefficients of different species are coupled. In particular, the diffusion of one variable may depend on the gradients of another variable. Cross-diffusion models have been used to study a wide range of phenomena, including pattern formation in biology, see
[
10
]
,
and chemistry, phase separation in materials science, and flow in porous media.
Sometimes, these systems are even coupled with the Navier-Stokes describing an underlying fluid.
Cross diffusion coupled with fluid dynamics
arises e.g. in fluid dynamics of mixtures, electro-chemistry, cell biology, and biofilm modelling.
For more details, we refer to
[
17
,
19
,
21
]
.

However, often these systems are not monotone or do not satisfy the maximum principle such that
standard techniques do not work, and one has to search for more sophisticated methods, especially if
a stochastic term is involved.
Here, often, one shows first the existence of a local solution. This solution is globalised using Galerkin method or another approximate scheme and showing that the
approximate solution is uniformly bounded by an entropy functional.
This has the disadvantage, that for each equation one has to design an approximate scheme, such that the entropy functional is preserved.
Our methods has the advantage that it does not involve an approximating scheme.
In this work, we first present a Meta Theorem by which nonlinear stochastic strongly coupled
parabolic systems can be solved. To demonstrate its applicability, we apply the Meta theorem to a chemotaxis system in porous media coupled with the Navier Stokes and show the existence of a martingale solution.

There are several recent works tackling systems of stochastic partial differential equations being coupled by nonlinearity and/or
cross-diffusion. Here, let us mention the work of Dahirwal et al.
[
7
]
, where the authors study cross-diffusion systems.
In addition, the first named author investigated in cooperation with different systems appearing in physical and biological applications, see
[
5
,
12
,
13
]
.

The rest of the paper is organized as follow. In Section 2, we state and prove a meta theorem for an existence result of nonlinear coupled systems and in Section 3, we prove the existence of a martingale solution to a stochastic porous media chemotaxis-fluid by verifying that the hypothesis of meta theorem obtained in section 2 are satisfied.

2.
The main Abstract result

Let
{\mathcal{O}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be an open domain,
d\geq 1
,
J\in\mathbb{N}
and

E,E_{1},...,E_{J}\subset{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}})
be the Hilbert spaces and
U,U_{1},...,U_{J}\subset{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}})
be the Banach spaces
1
1

1

Here,
{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}({\mathcal{O}})
denotes the space of Schwartz distributions on
{\mathcal{O}}
, that is, the topological dual space of smooth functions with compact support
{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{O}})=C_{0}^{\infty}({\mathcal{O}})
.
, such that

E\hookrightarrow U,\text{ and }E_{j}\hookrightarrow U_{j},\qquad j=1,...,J.

Let
\mathbb{X}\subset\{\eta:[0,T]\to U\}
be a Banach function space.

Let
{\mathfrak{A}}=(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
be a filtered probability space
with filtration
\mathbb{F}=({\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
satisfying the usual conditions. Let
H
be a separable Hilbert space and let
(W_{j}(t))_{t\in[0,T]}
,
j=0,...,J
be a cylindrical Wiener process
in
H
,
such that
W_{j}
has the representation

W(t)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\psi_{i}\beta^{j}_{i}(t),\quad t\in[0,T],

where
\{\psi_{i}:i\in\mathbb{I}\}
is a complete orthonormal system in
H
,
\mathbb{I}
a suitably chosen countable index set, and
\{\beta^{j}_{i}:i\in\mathbb{I}\}
a family of independent real-valued standard Brownian motions on
[0,T]
modeled in
{\mathfrak{A}}=(\Omega,{\mathcal{F}},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
. Due to
[
28
, Proposition 4.7, p. 85]
, this representation does not pose a restriction.

Here, we are interested in a solution
(w,v^{1},...,v^{J})
of the following system of Itô stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}dw(t)&=&\left(A_{0}w(t)+F_{0}(w(t),v^{1}(t),\cdots,v^{J}(t))\right)\,dt+\Sigma_{0}(w(t))\,dW_{0}(t),\\
dv^{j}(t)&=&\left(A_{j}v^{j}(t)+F_{j}(w(t),v^{j}(t),v^{j-1}(t),\cdots,v^{J}(t))\right)\,dt+\Sigma_{j}(v^{j}(t))\,dW_{j}(t),\\
&&\quad j=1,\ldots,J,\\
w(0)&=&w_{0},\\
v^{j}(0)&=&v^{j}_{0},\quad j=1,\ldots,J,\end{array}\right.

where the equalities hold respectively in
U_{j}
,
j=0,1,...,J
.

We shall also assume that
A_{0}:D(A_{0})\subset E_{0}\to E_{0}
is a possibly nonlinear and measurable (single-valued) operator,

A_{j}:D(A_{j})\subset E_{j}\to E_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,J
, are linear (single-valued) operators. The mappings
\Sigma_{j}
,
j=0,\ldots,J
, are densely defined mappings from
E_{j}
into
L_{HS}(H;E_{j})
specified later on
2
2

2

For a separable Hilbert space
H
and a any Hilbert space
E_{j}
defined
L_{HS}(H;E_{j})
the Hilbert-Schmidt operators from
H
to
E_{j}
.
and

F_{0}:E\times E_{1}\times\cdots\times E_{J}\longrightarrow U,\ \text{ and }\ F_{j}:E\times E_{j}\times\cdots\times E_{J}\longrightarrow U_{j},\quad j=1,...J,

are nonlinear mapping. Later on we will show in our examples that cross diffusions systems or reaction-diffusion equations will be covered.

For clarity, we define the notion of martingale solution to the system (
2
).

Definition 2.1
.

Fix
T>0
and initial conditions
\mathfrak{w}_{0}=(w_{0},v^{1}_{0},\ldots,v^{J}_{0})
. A martingale solution or weak solution to the problem
(
2
)
is given by the tuple

\left(\Omega,{{\mathcal{F}}},\mathbb{P},{\mathbb{F}},\mathbb{W},\mathfrak{w}\right)

such that

•

\mathfrak{A}:=(\Omega,{{\mathcal{F}}},{\mathbb{F}},\mathbb{P})
is a complete filtered
probability space with a filtration
{\mathbb{F}}=\{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{t}:t\in[0,T]\}
satisfying the usual conditions,

•

the vector-valued Wiener process

\mathbb{W}
is given by
\mathbb{W}=(W_{0},W_{1},\ldots,W_{J})
, where
W_{0},W_{1},

\ldots,W_{J}
are mutually independent cylindrical Wiener processes on
H
over the probability space

\mathfrak{A}
, and

•

the tuple
\mathfrak{w}=(w,v^{1},\ldots,v^{J})
is such that
w:[0,T]\times\Omega\rightarrow U
, and
v^{j}:[0,T]\times\Omega\rightarrow U_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,J

are
{{\mathbb{F}}}
–progressively measurable processes such that
{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s.,

(w,v^{1},\ldots,v^{J})\in C([0,T];U)\times C([0,T];U_{1})\times\cdot\cdot\cdot\times C([0,T];U_{J}),

and satisfy for all
t\in[0,T]
the following stochastic integral equations
{\mathbb{P}}
–a.s.

w(t)=w_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}(A_{0}w(s)+F(w(s),v^{1}(s),\ldots,v^{J}(s)\,)\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\Sigma_{0}(w(s))dW_{1}(s),\ \text{in }U,

(2.7)

and for
j=1,\ldots,J
,

v^{j}(t)=v^{j}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}(A_{j}v^{j}(s)+F_{j}(w(s),v^{j}(s),v^{j+1}(s),\ldots,v^{J}(s)\,)\,ds+\int_{0}^{t}\Sigma_{j}(v^{j}(s))dW_{j}(s),\ \text{in }U_{j}.

(2.8)

The main objective of this section is to prove an existence result for a solution of the abstract stochastic coupled system (
2
) in the sense of Definition
2.1
. A way to find a solution to this nonlinear system is using a Schauder fix point argument. In this way, for a certain fixed
m\geq 1
, we define the collection of processes

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}^{m}(\mathbb{X}):=

\displaystyle\Big\{\xi:\Omega\times[0,T]\times{{\mathcal{O}}}\to\mathbb{R}\;\colon\;

(2.9)

\displaystyle\text{$\xi$ is $\mathbb{F}$-progressively measurable}\;\text{and}\;\mathbb{E}|\xi|_{\mathbb{X}}^{m}<\infty\Big\}

equipped with the norm

|\xi|^{m}_{{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}^{m}(\mathbb{X})}:=\mathbb{E}|\xi|_{\mathbb{X}}^{m},\quad\xi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}^{m}(\mathbb{X}),

where

\mathbb{X}:=L^{m}(0,T;X)\subset\{\eta:[0,T]\to U\}

with
X
be a given UMD Banach space.

In order to overcome some difficulties caused by the non linearities of
F_{j}
, we first introduce a family of the cut-off functions. Let
\phi\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
be a smooth cut-off function that satisfies

\phi(x)\begin{cases}=0,&\mbox{ if }|x|\geq 2,\\
\in[0,1],&\mbox{ if }1<|x|<2,\\
=1,&\mbox{ if }|x|\leq 1,\end{cases}

and let

\phi_{\kappa}(x):=\phi(x/\kappa),\ x\in{\mathbb{R}},\,\kappa\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.10)

For
j=1,\ldots,J
, let
\mathbb{Y}_{j}
be a function space, e.g.
\mathbb{Y}_{j}=L^{m_{j}}(0,T;Y_{j})
or
\mathbb{Y}_{j}=C(0,T;Y_{j})
, with
1\leq m_{j}\leq\infty
and
Y_{j}
being some given UMD Banach spaces of type
2
. Let
h_{j}:\mathbb{Y}_{j}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{0}^{+}
be given by

h_{j}(\eta_{j},t)=\|\eta_{j}\mathds{1}_{[0,t)}\|_{\mathbb{Y}_{j}},\ \ \eta_{j}\in\mathbb{Y}_{j}.

Let

\Theta^{j}_{\kappa}(\eta_{j},t):=\phi_{\kappa}(h_{j}(\eta_{j},t))\ \text{ and }\ \Xi^{j}_{\kappa}(t):=\prod_{i=j}^{J}\Theta^{i}_{\kappa}(\eta_{j},t),\quad t\geq 0.

For any
\xi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}^{m}(\mathbb{X})
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
, we consider on the fixed filtered probability space
\mathfrak{A}=(\Omega,{{\mathcal{F}}},({{\mathcal{F}}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]},{\mathbb{P}})
given above, the following system

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}dw_{\kappa}(t)&=&\left(A_{0}w_{\kappa}(t)+\Xi^{1}_{\kappa}(t)\cdot F_{0}(\xi(t),v^{1}(t),\cdots,v^{J}(t))\,\right)\,dt+\Sigma_{0}(w_{\kappa}(t))\,dW_{0}(t),\ \text{in }U,\\
dv^{j}_{\kappa}(t)&=&\left(A_{j}v^{j}_{\kappa}(t)+\Xi^{j}_{\kappa}(t)\cdot F_{j}(\xi(t),v_{\kappa}^{j}(t),v_{\kappa}^{j+1}(t),\cdots,v_{\kappa}^{J}(t))\right)\,dt+\Sigma_{j}(v_{\kappa}^{j}(t))\,dW_{j}(t),\ \text{in }U_{j},\\
&&\quad j=1,\ldots,J,\\
w(0)&=&w_{0},\\
v^{j}(0)&=&v^{j}_{0},\quad j=1,\ldots,J.\end{array}\right.

Before we present the statement of the main abstract result, we outline shortly the idea. We implicitly assume that the equation (
2
) is well posed. Let us assume that the cut-off functions are chosen in such a way that for any cut-off parameter
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
,
there exists a bounded subset
\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{A}}^{\kappa}
of
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}^{m}(\mathbb{X})

such that for all
\xi\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{A}}^{\kappa}

there exists in the stochastic sense a unique strong solution
(w_{\kappa},v^{1}_{\kappa},...,v^{J}_{\kappa})
to the system (
2
) such that
w_{\kappa}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{A}}^{\kappa}
.
If we want to underline the dependence of
w_{\kappa}
or
v^{j}_{\kappa}
on
\xi
, we will write
w_{\kappa}(\xi)
or
v^{j}_{\kappa}(\xi)
, respectively.
we have to provide a bounded convex subset of
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}^{m}(\mathbb{X})
, such that the operator
{\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathfrak{A}},W,\eta}
will map this set into itself.
Here, it is essential to characterize the set in such a way that one can
transfer the definition to the set of probability measures.
This can be done in the following they. For each
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
,
we have to find two measurable functions
\Phi_{\kappa}:\mathbb{D}(0,T;U)\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
\Psi_{\kappa}:\mathbb{D}(0,T;U)\to{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}
such that for any bounded closed interval
I
the set
\Psi_{\kappa}^{\leftarrow}(I)
3
3

3

For a measurable function
f:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
f^{\leftarrow}
denotes the preimage
given by
\{x\in X:f(x)\in A\}
for all
A\in{{\mathcal{B}}}(I)
.
is closed in
\mathbb{X}
.
Let us now define for any
R>0

a subset

\mathcal{X}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R)
of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{A}}^{m}(\mathbb{X})
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\{\xi\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathfrak{A}}^{m}(\mathbb{X}):\mathbb{E}\Phi_{\kappa}(\xi)\leq R^{m}\mbox{ and }{\mathbb{P}}\left(\Psi_{\kappa}(\xi)<\infty\right)=1\right\}.

(2.16)

The set
\mathcal{X}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R)
has to be chosen in such a way that for all
R>0
the system (
2
) has a unique strong solution (in the stochastic sense)
w\in{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}^{m}(\mathbb{X})
for all
\xi\in{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}^{m}(\mathbb{X})\cap\mathcal{X}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R)
.

In the next, on the fixed filtered probability space
\mathfrak{A}=(\Omega,{{\mathcal{F}}},({{\mathcal{F}}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]},{\mathbb{P}})
given above, for a vector valued Wiener process
\mathbb{W}=(W_{0},W_{1},\ldots,W_{J})
, we then define the operator

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}^{k}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}:\mathcal{X}_{\mathfrak{A}}^{\kappa}(R)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}^{m}(\mathbb{X})

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}^{k}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi):=w_{\kappa}(\xi),

where
w_{\kappa}(\xi)
should be the first component of the solution
(w_{\kappa}(\xi),v^{1}_{\kappa}(\xi),...,v^{J}_{\kappa}(\xi))
of system (
2
).
Secondly, we have to find a sequence
\{R_{\kappa}:\kappa\in\mathbb{N}\}
, such that we have
\mathcal{X}^{\kappa-1}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R_{\kappa-1})\subset\mathcal{X}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R_{\kappa})
and

\mathcal{X}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R_{\kappa})
is invariant for
{\mathcal{V}}^{k}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}
for any
\kappa\geq 1
.
For simplicity let us put
\mathcal{X}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}:=\mathcal{X}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R_{\kappa})
.

Let us assume that
w_{\kappa}
is a fixed point of the system (
2
) on a new filtered probability space
\mathfrak{A}_{\kappa}=(\Omega_{\kappa},{{\mathcal{F}}}_{\kappa},({{\mathcal{F}}}^{\kappa}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]},{\mathbb{P}}_{\kappa})
with respect to the new Wiener process
\mathbb{W}_{\kappa}=(W^{\kappa}_{0},W^{\kappa}_{1},\ldots,W^{\kappa}_{J})
,. Then, on the time interval

[0,\tau^{\ast}_{\kappa})
, where

\displaystyle\tau^{\ast}_{\kappa}:=\min_{1\leq j\leq J}\tau^{j}_{\kappa}\quad\mbox{with}\quad\tau^{j}_{\kappa}=\tau^{j}_{\kappa}(v^{j}_{\kappa}):=\inf_{t>0}\{h_{j}(v^{j}_{\kappa},t)\geq\kappa\},\,\,j=1,\ldots,J,

(2.17)

the tuple
\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa}=(w_{\kappa},v_{\kappa}^{1}(w_{\kappa}),\ldots,v^{J}_{\kappa}(w_{\kappa}))
solves the original system (
2
). This means that, in addition to the new probability space and the new Wiener process,
\{(\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa},\tau_{\kappa}^{\ast})\}_{\kappa\geq 0}
is a family of local martingale solution of problem (
2
). The next step is to extend the local solution to a global solution. This will be done by finding some Lyapunov functional

\Lambda:\mathbb{Y}_{1}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{Y}_{J}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{+}_{0}

such that there exists some
p>0
and
C>0
with

\mathbb{E}\left|\Lambda(v^{1}(w_{\kappa}),v^{2}(w_{\kappa}),\ldots,v^{J}(w_{\kappa}))\right|^{p}\leq C,\quad\forall\,\kappa\in\mathbb{N}.

Here, it is important, that the inner part of the cut-off function is bounded by the functional. In particular, we need that there exists some
\delta>0
such that we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\,h_{j}(v^{j}_{\kappa},T)^{\delta}\leq C\mathbb{E}\,\Lambda(w_{\kappa},v^{1}(w_{\kappa}),v^{2}(w_{\kappa}),\ldots,v^{J}(w_{\kappa})),\quad\forall\,\kappa\in\mathbb{N},\,\text{ and }j=1,\ldots J.

(2.18)

If (
2.18
) is satisfied, then one can show by the Chebyscheff inequality that
the probability of the set

\Omega_{\kappa}:=\{\omega\in\Omega:\tau^{\ast}_{\kappa}\leq T\}

converges to zero as
\kappa
tends to infinity. This implies that

{\mathbb{P}}(\liminf_{\kappa\to\infty}\Omega_{\kappa})\leq\lim_{\kappa\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\kappa})=0,

where

\liminf_{\kappa\to\infty}\Omega_{\kappa}=\cup_{\kappa=0}^{\infty}\cap_{j=\kappa}^{\infty}\Omega_{j}.

Hence, we know that the set
\Omega_{0}:=\Omega\setminus\liminf_{k}\Omega_{\kappa}

has probability one and
the system with cut-off coincides on
\Omega_{0}
with the original equation.
In this way, the existence of a global martingale solution is proven.

We are now ready to formulate our main theorem.

Theorem 2.2

(Meta-Theorem)
.

Let
m>1
be given. Let us assume that for any
R>0
and
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a
bounded and convex set

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R):=\left\{\xi\in{{\mathcal{M}}}^{m}_{\mathfrak{A}}(\mathbb{X}):\mathbb{E}\Phi^{\kappa}(\xi)\leq R,\,{\mathbb{P}}\left(\Psi_{\kappa}(\xi)<\infty\right)=1\right\}

(2.19)

in
{{\mathcal{M}}}^{m}_{\mathfrak{A}}(\mathbb{X})
, such that
for all
\xi\in{{\mathcal{M}}}^{m}_{\mathfrak{A}}(\mathbb{X})
the system (
2
) has a unique probabilistic strong solution

\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa}(\xi)=({w}_{\kappa}(\xi),{v}^{1}_{\kappa}(\xi),\dots,{v}_{\kappa}^{J}(\xi))\in\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}_{1}\times...\times\mathbb{X}_{J},\ \mathbb{P}\text{-a.s},

where
\mathbb{X}_{j}\subset\{\eta:[0,T]\to U_{j}\}
,
j=1,...,J
, are given Banach spaces. In addition we assume that for any
R>0
we have

{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa-1}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R)\subset{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R)
.

Finally, we assume that there exists some initial condition given as follows:
there exists some Banach spaces
E_{0}
and
E_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,J
and measurable functions
g_{0}:E_{0}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
g_{j}:E_{j}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
j=1,\ldots,J
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}g_{0}(w_{0}),\mathbb{E}g_{1}(v^{1}_{0}),\ldots,\mathbb{E}g(v^{J}_{0})<\infty.

(2.20)

We then consider the following operator

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}:{{\mathcal{M}}}^{m}_{\mathfrak{A}}(\mathbb{X})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}^{m}(\mathbb{X})

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\longmapsto

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi):=w_{\kappa}(\xi),

where
w_{\kappa}(\xi)
is the first component of
({w}_{\kappa}(\xi),{v}^{1}_{\kappa}(\xi),\dots,{v}_{\kappa}^{J}(\xi))
solution of system (
2
).

If for any
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
,
there exists two measurable functions
\Phi_{\kappa}:\mathbb{X}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\Psi_{\kappa}:\mathbb{X}\to{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{\infty\}
such that

(a)

for any
R>0
the operator
{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}
is well posed on
{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R)
; in particular,
for all
\xi\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R_{\kappa})
, there exists a unique strong solution
\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa}=(w_{\kappa}(\xi),v^{1}_{\kappa}(\xi),...,v^{J}_{\kappa}(\xi))
of system (
2
) exists, with
{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)=w_{\kappa}
, and
w_{\kappa}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}^{m}(\mathbb{X})
.

(b)

for any
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a number
R_{\kappa}>0
such that the operator
{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}
maps
{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R)
into itself for any
R\geq R_{\kappa}
;

(c)

for any
R\geq R_{\kappa}
, the operator
{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}
restricted to
{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R)
is continuous on
{\mathcal{M}}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}^{m}(\mathbb{X})
.

(d)

for any
R\geq R_{\kappa}
, there exists a number
m_{0}>0
and a space
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{X}
compactly and there exists a
C_{0}>0
with

\mathbb{E}\|w_{\kappa}(\xi)\|_{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}^{m_{0}}\leq C_{0}
for all
\xi\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R)
;

(e)

for any
R\geq R_{\kappa}
, there exists a number
m_{1}>m
and a constant
C_{1}>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\|w_{\kappa}(\xi)\|_{\mathbb{X}}^{m_{1}}\leq C_{1}
for all
\xi\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R)
;

(f)

for any
R\geq R_{\kappa}
, we have
\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s}
,
({w}_{\kappa}(\xi),{v}^{1}_{\kappa}(\xi),\dots,{v}_{\kappa}^{J}(\xi))\in C([0,T];U)\times C([0,T];U_{1})\times\dots\times C([0,T];U_{J}),
for all
\xi\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R)
.

Let us assume that for any
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
a probability space
\mathfrak{A}_{\kappa}
, a Wiener process
\mathbb{W}_{\kappa}
defined on
\mathfrak{A}_{\kappa}
, and an element
w_{\kappa}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\kappa}}(R_{\kappa})
,
such that for all initial conditions being
{{\mathcal{F}}}_{0}
-measurable we have
{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\kappa}}(w_{\kappa})=w_{\kappa}
.
Let us assume that the condition on the initial data are preserved, i.e. at time
\tau^{\ast}_{\kappa}
(defined in (
2.17
)) the random variable
w_{\kappa}(\tau^{\ast}_{\kappa}),v^{1}_{\kappa}(\tau^{\ast}_{\kappa}),\ldots,v^{1}_{\kappa}(\tau^{\ast}_{\kappa})
satisfy condition (
2.20
).

If for the corresponding solution

\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa}=({w}_{\kappa},{v}^{1}_{\kappa},\dots,{v}_{\kappa}^{J}),

of the system (
2
),
there exists
m_{j}>1
,
j=1,\ldots,J
, and a constant
C(T)>0
(both independent of
\kappa
)
such that

\displaystyle\max_{j=1,\ldots,J}\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\left[h^{m_{j}}_{j}(v^{j}_{\kappa},t)\right]\leq C(T),\quad t\in[0,T],\;\kappa\in\mathbb{N},

(2.21)

then there exists a global martingale solution to the problem
(
2
) in the sense of Definition
2.1
.

Remark 2.3
.

Showing the uniform bound for the cut-off functions, it is essential that
to find a Lyapunov type functional
\Lambda
, such that
h_{j}(v^{j}_{\kappa},T)
are bounded by the Lyapunov functional.
In particular, one has to show that

\mathbb{E}h^{m_{j}}_{j}(v^{j}_{\kappa}(\xi),T)\leq\mathbb{E}\Lambda(\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa}(\xi)),\quad j=1,\ldots,J,

and

\mathbb{E}\Lambda(\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa}(\xi))\leq C.

Now, we give a proof of Theorem
2.2
.

Proof.

As outlined before, the proof of the Theorem
2.2
consists of several steps. Part of the steps we have already introduced in the first paragraphs, as well as part of the ingredients, like the cut-off function
\phi
, the mappings
h
and
\Theta
, the system (
2
), the operator
\mathcal{V}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},W}
, and the corresponding bounded and convex sets
\mathcal{X}_{\kappa}
on which the operator
\mathcal{V}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},W}
acting on.

Step (I)

In this step we will show that for any
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a fix point and that the fix point solves the system given in (
2
) on a new probability space.
In particular, one can get for each
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
a local martingale solution.

Claim 2.4
.

For any
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a probability space

\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}_{\kappa}=(\tilde{\Omega}_{\kappa},\tilde{{{\mathcal{F}}}}_{\kappa},\tilde{\mathbb{F}}_{\kappa},\tilde{{\mathbb{P}}}_{\kappa}),

a vector valued Wiener process

\tilde{\mathbb{W}}^{\kappa}=(\tilde{W}_{0}^{\kappa},\tilde{W}_{1}^{\kappa},\ldots,\tilde{W}^{\kappa}_{J})

being defined on
\tilde{\mathfrak{A}}_{\kappa}
, and a tuple of random variable

\tilde{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa}:=(\tilde{w}_{\kappa},\tilde{v}^{j}_{\kappa},\ldots,\tilde{v}^{J}_{\kappa})\in C([0,T];U)\times C([0,T];U_{1})\times\cdots\times C([0,T];U_{J}),\ \tilde{{\mathbb{P}}}_{\kappa}-a.s.

solving the system (
2
)
\tilde{{\mathbb{P}}}_{\kappa}
-a.s.

Proof of Claim
2.4
.

The proof is closed to the prove of the stochastic Schauder Tychonoff Theorem given in
[
14
]
.
In
[
11
]
a refined version with a Lévy process is given. However, our main difference is that we have only to proof the assumption for the existing fixpoint for one process, i.e.
w_{\kappa}
. Here, it is essential that only a dependence from equation for
v_{\kappa}^{j}
to
v_{\kappa}^{j-1}
exists.

Step (a)

In this step we will approximate the operator
{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}
via a time discretisation. To this aim, in a similar way as in
[
4
]
, for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
we introduce a dyadic time grid

\pi:=\left\{\frac{iT}{2^{\ell}};\ \ i=0,1,...,2^{\ell}-1\right\},

and define the shifted Haar projection
\mbox{Proj}_{\ell}
of order
\ell
by for
f\in\mathbb{X}
such that
f(0)
exists,

(\mbox{Proj}_{\ell}f)(s):=\begin{cases}f(0),\ \text{if }\ s\in[0,\frac{T}{2^{\ell}})\\
\frac{2^{\ell}}{T}\int_{\frac{iT}{2^{\ell}}}^{\frac{(i+1)T}{2^{\ell}}}f(r-\frac{T}{2^{\ell}})\>dr,\text{ if }\ s\in[\frac{iT}{2^{\ell}},\frac{(i+1)T}{2^{\ell}}),\ i=1,2,3...,2^{\ell}-1.\end{cases}

Note, that if
f\in{{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathfrak{A}}^{m}(\mathbb{X})
is such that
f(0)
exists, then
\mbox{Proj}_{\ell}f
is a
progressively measurable, piecewise constant,
X
-valued
process.

Remark 2.5
.

We recall in
[
4
, Section B]
, that for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,

(a)

\mbox{Proj}_{\ell}
is a bounded linear operator from
\mathbb{X}
into
\mathbb{X}
. In fact

\|\mbox{Proj}_{\ell}f\|_{\mathbb{X}}\leq\|f\|_{\mathbb{X}},\quad\forall f\in\mathbb{X}.

(b)

if
K
is a bounded compact subset of
\mathbb{X}
,
then for all
\varepsilon>0
there exists a
\ell_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\|\mbox{Proj}_{\ell}f-f\|_{\mathbb{X}}\leq\varepsilon,\quad f\in K,\quad\forall\ell\geq\ell_{\varepsilon}.

(c)

\mbox{Proj}_{\ell}
is convergence in
\mathbb{X}
.

\mbox{Proj}_{\ell}f\longrightarrow f\quad\text{as}\quad\ell\longrightarrow\infty\quad\text{ in }\quad\mathbb{X}.

It follows that for all
\varepsilon>0
there exists a
\ell_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mathbb{E}\|\mbox{Proj}_{\ell}{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)-{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\|_{\mathbb{X}}\leq\varepsilon,\quad\forall\,\xi\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R_{\kappa})\quad\forall\ell\geq\ell_{\varepsilon}.

Indeed let us fix
\varepsilon>0
. Due to the assumption (
d
), we know that there exists
C_{0}>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\|{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\|_{\mathbb{X}}^{m_{0}}\leq C_{0},\quad\forall\xi\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R_{\kappa}).

Let us consider
m^{\prime}_{0}>0
is such that
\frac{1}{m_{0}}+\frac{1}{m^{\prime}_{0}}=1
and
R_{\varepsilon}>0
such that

2C\left(\frac{C_{0}}{R^{m_{0}}_{\varepsilon}}\right)^{\frac{1}{m^{\prime}_{0}}}\,\left(C_{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{m_{0}}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2},

where
C>0
is a constant coming from the continue embedding of
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
into
\mathbb{X}
. We then consider the set

K_{\varepsilon}=\left\{\xi\in\mathbb{X}:\|\xi\|_{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}\leq R_{\varepsilon}\right\}.

Since
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\hookrightarrow\mathbb{X}
compactly, the set
K_{\varepsilon}
is a compact set of
\mathbb{X}
. Hence, invoking the Chebycheff inequality we derive that

\displaystyle\left({\mathbb{P}}\left({\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\not\in K_{\varepsilon}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{m^{\prime}_{0}}}\,\left(\mathbb{E}\|{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\|_{\mathbb{X}}^{m_{0}}\right)^{\frac{1}{m_{0}}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left({\mathbb{P}}\left(\|{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\|_{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}>R_{\varepsilon}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{m^{\prime}_{0}}}\,\left(\mathbb{E}\|{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\|_{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}^{m_{0}}\right)^{\frac{1}{m_{0}}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}\|{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\|^{m_{0}}_{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}}{R^{m_{0}}_{\varepsilon}}\right)^{\frac{1}{m^{\prime}_{0}}}\,\left(C_{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{m_{0}}}.

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\frac{C_{0}}{R^{m_{0}}_{\varepsilon}}\right)^{\frac{1}{m^{\prime}_{0}}}\,\left(C_{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{m_{0}}}.

Let
\ell_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{N}
large such that

\|\mbox{Proj}_{\ell}f-f\|_{\mathbb{X}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\quad\forall f\in K_{\varepsilon},\text{and for all }\ell\geq\ell_{\varepsilon}.

Using the first item of Remark
2.5
, we note that for
\ell\geq\ell_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|\mbox{Proj}_{\ell}{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)-{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\|_{\mathbb{X}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}1_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\in K_{\varepsilon}}\|\mbox{Proj}_{\ell}{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)-{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\|_{\mathbb{X}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}1_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\not\in K_{\varepsilon}}\left(\|\mbox{Proj}_{\ell}{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\|_{\mathbb{X}}+\|{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\|_{\mathbb{X}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+2\mathbb{E}1_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\not\in K_{\varepsilon}}\|{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\|_{\mathbb{X}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+2C\left(\mathbb{E}1_{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},W}(\xi)\not\in K_{\varepsilon}}^{m^{\prime}_{0}}\right)^{\frac{1}{m^{\prime}_{0}}}\,\left(\mathbb{E}\|{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\|_{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}^{m_{0}}\right)^{\frac{1}{m_{0}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+2C\left({\mathbb{P}}\left({\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\not\in K_{\varepsilon}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{m^{\prime}_{0}}}\,\left(\mathbb{E}\|{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)\|_{\mathbb{X}}^{m_{0}}\right)^{\frac{1}{m_{0}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+2C\left(\frac{C_{0}}{R^{m_{0}}_{\varepsilon}}\right)^{\frac{1}{m^{\prime}_{0}}}\,\left(C_{0}\right)^{\frac{1}{m_{0}}}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\forall\xi\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R_{\kappa}).

Finally, let us define the operators

{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi):=\mbox{Proj}_{\ell}{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi),\quad\xi\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R_{\kappa}).

Step (b)

For any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
the family of operators
\{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}:\ell\in\mathbb{N}\}
induces a family of operators
\{\mathscr{V}^{\kappa,\ell}:\ell\in\mathbb{N}\}
on the set of Borel probability measures on
\mathbb{X}
denoted by
\mathscr{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})
4
4

4

Let
(E,d)
be a metric space. Let
\mathscr{M}_{1}(E)
be the set of Borel probability measures on the metric space
(X,d)
. Note, that the KyFun metric is defined in the following (see
[
8
, Theorem 11.3.1]
): For
\nu,\mu\in\mathscr{M}_{1}(X)
,

d_{K}(\mu,\nu):=\inf\{\alpha>0:\mu(A)\leq\nu(A_{\alpha})+\alpha\mbox{ and }\nu(A)\leq\mu(A_{\alpha})+\alpha\mbox{ for all }A\in{{\mathcal{B}}}(X)\}.

Here
A_{\alpha}:=\{x\in X:d(x,A)<\alpha\}
. The Ky Fan and Prohorov metric and the Prohorov metric characterizes weak convergence (see
[
8
, Theorem 11.3.3]
).
Note that if
(S,d)
is a separable complete metric space, then the set of all laws on
S
defined on
{{\mathcal{B}}}(S)
is complete for the Prohorov or KyFan metric
d_{K}
, see
[
8
, Corollary 11.5.5]
.
. The construction of
\mathscr{V}^{\kappa,\ell}
is done in the following way. Firstly, let us observe that due to assumption (
f
), for all
\xi\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}}(R_{\kappa})
the process
{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\xi)
belongs
{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s. to
\mathbb{D}(0,T,U)
. In fact, due to the point (
f
),
{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell}_{{\mathfrak{A}},\mathbb{W}}(\cdot)
maps
\xi
into
C(0,T;U)
and the projection
\mbox{Proj}_{\ell}
projects
C(0,T;U)
onto
\mathbb{D}(0,T,U)
. Hence, let us define the subset of probability measures
\mathscr{X}_{\kappa}
given by

\displaystyle\mathscr{X}_{\kappa}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\{\mathscr{Q}\in\mathscr{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X}\cap\mathbb{D}(0,T;U)):\int_{\mathbb{X}}\Psi(x)d\mathscr{Q}\leq R_{\kappa}\right\}.

(2.22)

Let
\mathscr{Q}
be a probability measure belonging to
\mathscr{X}_{\kappa}
.
Then, by the Skorohod theorem, there exists a
probability space
\mathfrak{A}_{0}=(\Omega_{0},{{\mathcal{F}}}^{0},{\mathbb{P}}_{0})
and a random variable

\xi:\Omega_{0}\to\mathbb{X}\cap\mathbb{D}(0,T;U)
such that the law of
\xi
coincides with
\mathscr{Q}
.
In particular,
the probability measure
\mathscr{P}_{\xi}:{{\mathcal{B}}}(\mathbb{X})\to[0,1]
induced by
\xi
and given by

\mathscr{P}_{\xi}:{{\mathcal{B}}}(\mathbb{X})\ni A\mapsto{\mathbb{P}}_{0}\left(\left\{\omega:\xi(\omega)\in A\right\}\right)

coincides with the probability measure
\mathscr{Q}
.
Since
{\mathbb{P}}
-a.s.,
\xi\in\mathbb{D}(0,T;U)
we know, that for any
t\in[0,T]
,
\xi(t)
is a
U
–valued random variable over
\mathfrak{A}_{0}
.
In particular, the mapping
\xi:\Omega_{0}\times[0,T]\to U
is well defined.
Let

{{\mathcal{F}}}^{0}_{t}:=\sigma\left(\left\{\,\xi(s),\;\colon\;0\leq s\leq t\,\right\}\cup\tilde{{\mathcal{N}}}_{0}\right),\quad t\in[0,T],

where
{{\mathcal{N}}}_{0}
denotes the zero sets of
{\mathfrak{A}}_{0}
.
Let us put
\mathfrak{A}_{0}:=(\Omega_{0},{{\mathcal{F}}}^{0},({{\mathcal{F}}}_{t}^{0})_{t\in[0,T]},{\mathbb{P}}_{0})
.

Next, we have to construct the
J+1
-tuple of Wiener processes and extend the probability space
\mathfrak{A}_{0}
.
Then, we consider the filtered probability space
\mathfrak{A}_{1}=(\Omega_{1},{{\mathcal{F}}}^{1},({{\mathcal{F}}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]},{\mathbb{P}}_{1})
where on the cylindrical Wiener processes
\mathbb{W}=(W_{0},\ldots,W_{J})
is defined, and let
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}}
the product probability space of
\mathfrak{A}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
.
In particular, we put

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathscr{Q}}=\Omega_{0}\times\Omega_{1},

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{F}}}_{\mathscr{Q}}={{\mathcal{F}}}^{0}\otimes{{\mathcal{F}}}^{1},

\displaystyle{{\mathcal{F}}}^{\mathscr{Q}}_{t}={{\mathcal{F}}}^{0}_{t}\otimes{{\mathcal{F}}}^{1}_{t},\quad

and

\displaystyle\quad{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathscr{Q}}={\mathbb{P}}_{0}\times{\mathbb{P}}_{1}.

Here, we know that
\xi
at time
t
is independent of the increments of the Wiener process after time t. In particular, the
integral w.r.t. the Wiener process is an Ito integral.
By the choice of
\mathscr{X}_{\kappa}
, we know that
\xi\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}}}(R_{\kappa})
and therefore,
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}},\mathbb{W}}^{\kappa,\ell}(\xi)
is well defined with

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}}=(\Omega_{\mathscr{Q}},{{\mathcal{F}}}_{\mathscr{Q}},({{\mathcal{F}}}^{\mathscr{Q}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]},{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathscr{Q}}).

Let us define a family of operators

\{\mathscr{V}^{\kappa,\ell}:\ell\in\mathbb{N}\}
acting on
\mathscr{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})
and
having the same law as
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}},\mathbb{W}}^{\kappa,\ell}(\xi)
.
To be more precise, the mapping

\mathscr{V}^{\kappa,\ell}:\mathscr{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})\longrightarrow\mathscr{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})

maps
the probability measure
\mathscr{P}_{\xi}:{{\mathcal{B}}}(\mathbb{X})\to[0,1]
induced by
\xi
(the random variable
\xi
is obtained via a given probability measure
\mathscr{Q}\in\mathscr{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})
) to the probability measure
\mathscr{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}},\mathbb{W}}^{\kappa,\ell}(\xi)}:{{\mathcal{B}}}(\mathbb{X})\to[0,1]
given by

\mathscr{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}},\mathbb{W}}^{\kappa,\ell}(\xi)}(A):={\mathbb{P}}_{\mathscr{Q}}\left(\left\{\omega\in\Omega_{\mathscr{Q}}:{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}},\mathbb{W}}^{\kappa,\ell}(\xi(\omega))\in A\right\}\right),\ \forall A\in{{\mathcal{B}}}(\mathbb{X}).

Note, since
\mathbb{X}
is a separable Banach space,
the metric space of probability measures over
\mathbb{X}
equipped with the
Prohorov metric is separable and complete.

The following points are valid:

(i)

For a fixed
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
, due to the fact that
{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}_{\mathscr{Q}},\mathbb{W}}
restricted to
{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}}}(R_{\kappa})
is continuous (see point (
c
)) and the fact that the family
\{\mathscr{V}^{\kappa,\ell},\ell\in\mathbb{N}\}
is uniformly continuous, the family of operator
\{\text{Proj}_{\ell},\ell\in\mathbb{N}\}
restricted to
\mathscr{X}_{\kappa}
is uniformly continuous on
\mathscr{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})
in the Prohorov metric. This point follows from
[
8
, Lemma 11.7.1]
.

(ii)

\mathscr{V}^{\kappa,\ell}
restricted to
\mathscr{X}_{\kappa}
is compact on
\mathscr{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})
.
In particular, it maps bounded sets into compact sets.
In fact, we have to show that for all
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon>0
there exists a compact subset in
{{\mathcal{B}}}(\mathbb{X})
such that

\mathscr{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}},\mathbb{W}}^{\kappa,\ell}(\xi)}\left(\mathbb{X}\setminus K_{\varepsilon}\right)\leq\varepsilon,\quad\forall\mathscr{Q}=\mathscr{P}_{\xi}\in\mathscr{K}_{\kappa}\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\mathbb{P}}_{{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}},\mathbb{W}}^{\kappa,\ell}(\xi)}:=\mathscr{V}^{\kappa,\ell}(\mathscr{P}_{\xi}).

Indeed, from assumption (
d
) we know that there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\|{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}},\mathbb{W}}^{\kappa,\ell}(\xi)\|_{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}^{m_{0}}\leq C,\quad\forall\xi\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}}}(R_{\kappa}).

Let
\tilde{R}>0
so large that

\frac{C}{\tilde{R}^{m_{0}}}\leq\varepsilon

and let
K_{\varepsilon}:=\{f\in\mathbb{X}:\|x\|_{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}\leq\tilde{R}\}
.
Note, we know by the Chebyscheff inequality that
we
have

\mathscr{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}},\mathbb{W}}^{\kappa,\ell}(\xi)}\left(\mathbb{X}\setminus K_{\varepsilon}\right)=\mathscr{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}},\mathbb{W}}^{\kappa,\ell}(\xi)}\left(\overline{\left\{f\in\mathbb{X}:\|x\|_{\mathbb{X}^{\prime}}\geq\tilde{R}\right\}}\right)\leq\epsilon

for all
\mathscr{P}_{\xi}\in\mathscr{K}_{\kappa}
where
\mathscr{P}_{{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}},\mathbb{W}}^{\kappa,\ell}(\xi)}:=\mathscr{V}^{\kappa,\ell}(\mathscr{P}_{\xi})
.
Since
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{X}
compactly,
we have proven the tightness and the Prohorov theorem gives compactness on
\mathscr{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})
.

(iii)

\mathscr{X}_{\kappa}
is a convex subset of
\mathscr{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})
and is invariant under
\mathscr{V}^{\kappa,\ell}
. In fact the convexity follows by direct calculations and the invariance follows from the assumption (
b
). Let us consider the probability measure
\mathscr{Q}\in\mathscr{M}_{1}(\mathbb{X})
. Then there exists a filtered probability space
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}}
, the Wiener process
\mathbb{W}
and we can construct a bounded set
{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}}}(R_{\kappa})
such that
{\mathcal{V}}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}},\mathbb{W}}^{\kappa,\ell}
maps
{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathscr{Q}}}(R_{\kappa})
into itself. Since
\|\mbox{Proj}_{\ell}\xi\|_{\mathbb{X}}\leq\|\xi\|_{\mathbb{X}},
we then know that
\mathscr{X}_{\kappa}
is invariant under
\mathscr{V}^{\kappa,\ell}
.

In particular, the mapping
\mathscr{V}^{\kappa,\ell}
restricted to
\mathscr{X}_{\kappa}

satisfies the assumption of the Schauder-Tychonov Theorem (see
[
Granas
, Theorem 1.13]
).
Hence, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a probability measure
\mathscr{P}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell}
such that
\mathscr{V}^{\kappa,\ell}(\mathscr{P}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell})=\mathscr{P}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell}
.

Step (c)

Note, for a fixed
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
, since the estimate on
\mathbb{X}^{\prime}
is uniform, the set

\left\{\mathscr{P}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell}\ :\ \ell\in\mathbb{N}\right\}

is tight. Hence, there exists a subsequence

\{\ell_{j}:j\in\mathbb{N}\}
and a Borel probability measure
\mathscr{P}^{\ast}_{\kappa}
such that

\mathscr{P}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}\to\mathscr{P}_{\kappa}^{\ast}
, as
j\to\infty
. By the Skorokhod lemma
[
18
, Theorem 4.30]
,
there exists a probability space
\mathfrak{A}^{\ast}_{0}=(\Omega^{\ast}_{0},{{\mathcal{F}}}^{\ast}_{0},{\mathbb{P}}^{\ast}_{0})
and a sequence of
\mathbb{X}
-valued random variables
\{{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}:j\in\mathbb{N}\}

and
{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa}
where
the random variable
w^{\ast}_{\kappa}:\Omega^{\ast}_{0}\to\mathbb{X}
has the law
\mathscr{P}^{\ast}_{\kappa}
and

\displaystyle\text{Law}({w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}})=\mathscr{P}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}^{\ast},\quad j\in\mathbb{N},

(2.23)

and such that

{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}\to{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa}\quad\mbox{ as $j\to\infty$ }\,\,{\mathbb{P}}^{\ast}_{0}\text{-a.s.}\text{ on }\mathbb{X}.

(2.24)

Next, we have to construct the Wiener process.
Let us first consider the filtered probability space

\mathfrak{A}_{1}=\left(\Omega_{1},{\mathbb{P}}_{1},{{\mathcal{F}}}_{1},({{\mathcal{G}}}^{1}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}\right),

where on the cylindrical Wiener processes
\mathbb{W}=(W_{0},\ldots,W_{J})
is defined. In particular, we put

\displaystyle\Omega^{\ast}=\Omega_{0}^{\ast}\times\Omega^{\ast}_{1},\quad{{\mathcal{F}}}^{\ast}={{\mathcal{F}}}_{0}^{\ast}\otimes{{\mathcal{F}}}_{1}^{\ast},\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\mathbb{P}}^{\ast}={\mathbb{P}}^{\ast}_{0}\otimes{\mathbb{P}}_{1}.

and extend the random variables
\mathbb{W}
,
{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}
on
(\Omega^{\ast},\mathcal{F}^{\ast},\mathbb{P}^{\ast})
as follows for all
t\in[0.T]
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}(t,\omega_{0},\omega_{1})=\mathbb{W}(t,\omega_{1}),\qquad\forall\ (\omega_{0},\omega_{1})\in\Omega^{\ast},

\displaystyle{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}(\omega_{0},\omega_{1})={w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}(\omega_{0}),\qquad\forall\ (\omega_{0},\omega_{1})\in\Omega^{\ast},

\displaystyle{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa}(\omega_{0},\omega_{1})={w}^{\ast}_{\kappa}(\omega_{0}),\qquad\forall\ (\omega_{0},\omega_{1})\in\Omega^{\ast}.

Moreover, let us introduce the filtration
{{\mathcal{G}}}^{\ast}=(\mathcal{G}_{t}^{\ast})_{t\in[0,T]}
given by

{{\mathcal{G}}}^{\ast}_{t}:=\sigma\left(\left\{\,(w_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}^{\ast}(s),w^{\ast}_{\kappa}(s),\mathbb{W}(s))\;\colon\;0\leq s\leq t\ ,\ j\in\mathbb{N}\right\}\cup\tilde{{\mathcal{N}}}_{0}\right),\quad t\in[0,T],

where
{{\mathcal{N}}}_{0}
denotes the zero sets of
(\Omega^{\ast},\mathcal{F}^{\ast},\mathbb{P}^{\ast})
. Let us set
\mathfrak{A}^{\ast}:=(\Omega^{\ast},\mathcal{F}^{\ast},{{\mathcal{G}}}^{\ast},\mathbb{P}^{\ast})
and recall that by the definition of the filtration
{{\mathcal{G}}}^{\ast}
, the process
\mathbb{W}
is Wiener process over
\mathfrak{A}^{\ast}
.

In addition,
{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast}}(R_{\kappa})
can be defined, and also the operators
{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}
and its projection
{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}
.

Step (d)

We start by remarking that although
\mathscr{V}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}(\mathscr{P}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}})=\mathscr{P}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}
, the process
w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}
, does not have any guaranties to satisfy

{\mathbb{P}}^{\ast}\left(\left\{{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}(w_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}^{\ast})(s)=w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}(s)\right\}\right)=1\quad\mbox{for}\quad 0\leq s\leq T.

In this step, we will construct a fixed point denoted by
\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}
to the operator
{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}
and that the probability measure will not change. We will construct this new process by induction. To start with let us note that since
\mathscr{V}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}(\mathscr{P}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}})=\mathscr{P}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}
, then by the equality (
2.23
) and the definition of
\mathscr{X}_{\kappa}
and
{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast}}(R_{\kappa})
, the process
w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast}}(R_{\kappa})
and hence,

{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}(w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}})

are well defined. Indeed for
\xi=w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}
, by the hypothesis of Meta theorem, considering the filtered probability space
\mathfrak{A}^{\ast}
, we can solve the system (
2
) to obtain the
J+1
-couples
({\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}(w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}),{v}^{1}_{\kappa}(w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}),\dots,{v}_{\kappa}^{J}(w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}))
such that by assumption (
f
),
\mathbb{P}^{\ast}\text{-a.s},

({\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}(w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}),{v}^{1}_{\kappa}(w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}),\dots,{v}_{\kappa}^{J}(w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}))\in C(0,T;U)\times C(0,T;U_{1})\times\dots\times C(0,T;U_{J}).

We also note that since
\mathscr{V}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}(\mathscr{P}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}})=\mathscr{P}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}
, we have

\mbox{Law}({\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}(w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}))=\mbox{Law}(w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}).

Now, we construct here a fixed point to the operator
{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}
. To start with, by induction let

\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},0}(s)=w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}(s),\qquad s\in[0,T],

and

\displaystyle\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},1}(s)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}(s)&\mbox{ if }0\leq s<t^{\ell_{j}}_{1},\\
\left({{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}(w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}})\right)(s)&\mbox{ if }t^{\ell_{j}}_{1}\leq s\leq T.\end{cases}

(2.25)

Clearly, in the time interval
[0,t_{1}^{\ell_{j}})
, the law is the same and by the fact that the operator
{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}
is invariant with respect to the measure
{\mathbb{P}}^{\ast}
, we will not change the law on the time interval
[t_{1}^{\ell_{j}},T]
.

Next, let

\displaystyle\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2}(s)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},1}(s)&\mbox{ if }0\leq s<t^{\ell_{j}}_{2},\\
\left({{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}(\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},1})\right)(s)&\mbox{ if }t^{\ell_{j}}_{2}\leq s\leq T.\end{cases}

(2.26)

Similar like before, by the fact that in the time interval
[0,t_{2}^{\ell_{j}})
, the law is the same and since the operator
{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}
is invariant with respect to the measure
{\mathbb{P}}^{\ast}
, we will not change the law on the time interval
[t_{2}^{\ell_{j}},T]
.

Now, having defined
\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},i}
,
i=1,2,3,...,2^{\ell_{j}}-1
, let

\displaystyle\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},i+1}(s)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},i}(s)&\mbox{ if }0\leq s<t^{\ell_{j}}_{i+1},\\
\left({{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}(\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},i})\right)(s)&\mbox{ if }t^{\ell_{j}}_{i+1}\leq s\leq T.\end{cases}

(2.27)

Our claim is now, that the process
\tilde{w}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}^{\ast}
satisfies

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}^{\ast}\left(\left\{{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}(\tilde{w}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}^{\ast})(s)=\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}(s)\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1\quad\mbox{for}\quad 0\leq s\leq T.

(2.28)

In fact, we have
{\mathbb{P}}^{\ast}
-a.s. by definition we have,

{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}\left(\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}\right)(s)={{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}\left(\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},0}\right)(s),\quad 0\leq s<t^{\ell_{j}}_{1}.

By the definition of
\mbox{Proj}_{\ell_{j}}
on
[0,t_{1}^{\ell_{j}})

the process on
[0,t_{1}^{\ell_{j}})
is defined by the initial data.
In particular, we have
{\mathbb{P}}^{\ast}
-a.s.

{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}\left(\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}\right)(s)=w^{\ast}_{0},\quad\mbox{for}\quad 0\leq s<t^{\ell_{j}}_{1}.

On the other side, we have

\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}(s)=w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}(s)=w^{\ast}_{0}\ \text{ for }\ 0\leq s<t^{\ell_{j}}_{1}.

At time
t^{\ell_{j}}_{1}
, we have by (
2.25
)

{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}\left(\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}\right)(t_{1}^{\ell_{j}})={{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}\left(\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}\right)(t_{1}^{\ell_{j}})=\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},1}(t^{\ell_{j}}_{1}).

However, by definition we have

\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}(t_{1}^{\ell_{j}})=\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},1}(t^{\ell_{j}}_{1}).

Now, we analyse what is happening at the next time interval
[t^{\ell_{j}}_{1},t^{\ell_{j}}_{2})
. Note that, here the process is constant and equals
{\mathbb{P}}^{\ast}
-a.s. the value at
t^{\ell_{j}}_{1}
, i.e.

{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}\left(\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}\right)(s)=\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},1}(s),\quad\mbox{for}\quad t^{\ell_{j}}_{1}\leq s<t^{\ell_{j}}_{2}.

Note, also that
{\mathbb{P}}^{\ast}
-a.s. we have
\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}(s)=\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},1}(s)
for
t^{\ell_{j}}_{1}\leq s<t^{\ell_{j}}_{2}
, and, hence

{\mathbb{P}}^{\ast}\left({{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}(\tilde{w}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}^{\ast})(s)=\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}(s)\right)=1,\quad\mbox{for}\quad t^{\ell_{j}}_{1}\leq s<t^{\ell_{j}}_{2}.

Next we analyse what happens in
t^{\ell_{j}}_{2}
. We note that by definition we have

{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}\left(\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}\right)(t_{2}^{\ell_{j}})={{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}\left(\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},1}\right)(t_{2}^{\ell_{j}})=\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2}(t^{\ell_{j}}_{2}),

and
\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}(t_{2}^{\ell_{j}})=\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2}(t^{\ell_{j}}_{2})
.

Now, by induction let us assume that in
[0,t^{\ell_{j}}_{i})
we have shown that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}^{\ast}\left({{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}(\tilde{w}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}^{\ast})(s)=\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}(s)\right)=1\quad\mbox{for}\quad 0\leq s\leq t^{\ell_{j}}_{i}.

(2.29)

Then, since by definition, on
t_{i}^{\ell_{j}}\leq s<t_{i+1}^{\ell_{j}}

{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}\left(\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}\right)(t_{i}^{\ell_{j}})={{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}\left(\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},i-1}\right)(t_{i}^{\ell_{j}})=\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},i}(t^{\ell_{j}}_{i}),

the conclusion follows by the fact that

\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}(t_{i}^{\ell_{j}})=\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},i}(t^{\ell_{j}}_{i})
.

Step (e)

Next, we verify the following statements with the goal to pass to the limit. We know due to the construction and the properties of the projection that

\text{Law}(\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}})=\text{Law}({w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}).

Then, by assumption (
e
) we note that

•

there exists a constant
C>0
such that
\sup_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathbb{E}^{\ast}\left[\|\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}\|^{m_{1}}_{\mathbb{X}}\right]\leq C
and

•

for any
r\in(m,m_{1})
we have

\lim_{j\to\infty}{{\mathbb{E}^{\ast}}}\left[\|\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}-w_{\kappa}^{\ast}\|_{\mathbb{X}}^{r}\right]=0.

Indeed, since
\{{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast}}(R_{\kappa})
and
{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast}}(R_{\kappa})
is bounded in
\mathbb{X}
,
we can conclude due to the equality of law that

\mathbb{E}^{\ast}\left\|\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}\right\|_{\mathbb{X}}^{r}={\mathbb{E}}^{\ast}\|{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}\|_{\mathbb{X}}^{r},

for any
r\in(m,m_{1}]
,
so that we get by assumption (
e
) that

\sup_{j}{\mathbb{E}}^{\ast}\|\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}\|_{\mathbb{X}}^{r}=\sup_{j}{\mathbb{E}}^{\ast}\|{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}\|_{\mathbb{X}}^{r}\leq CR,\qquad\forall r\in[1,m_{0}],

Hence, we know that
\{\|w^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}\|_{\mathbb{X}}^{r}\}

is uniformly integrable for any
r\in(m,m_{1}]
w.r.t. the probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{\ast}
.
Moreover, by the convergence (
2.24
), we get by the Vitali convergence theorem that

\lim_{j\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}^{\ast}\left\|{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j}}-{w}_{\kappa}^{\ast}\right\|_{\mathbb{X}}^{r}=0

for any
r\in(m,m_{1}]
. By the equality of the laws, we then infer that

\lim_{j\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}^{\ast}\left\|\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}-{w}_{\kappa}^{\ast}\right\|_{\mathbb{X}}^{r}=0.

Step (f)

In this step we show that
w_{\kappa}^{\ast}={\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast})
and derive that over
{\mathfrak{A}}^{\ast}
, the random variable
(w_{\kappa}^{\ast},v_{\kappa}^{1}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast}),...,v^{J}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast}))
together with the Wiener process
\mathbb{W}
is indeed a martingale solution to (
2
) where
v_{\kappa}^{1}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast}),...,v_{\kappa}^{J}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast})
is obtained via
{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast})
.
We have the following decomposition for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

\displaystyle w_{\kappa}^{\ast}-{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{w_{\kappa}^{\ast}-\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}}_{:=I}+\underbrace{\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}-{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}(\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}})}_{=:II}+\underbrace{{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}(\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}})-{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast})}_{=:III}+\underbrace{{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast})-{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{{\mathfrak{A}}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast})}_{=:IV}.

Now, we analyse
I
,
II
,
III
, and
IV
.

Note, due to the convergence we know

\mathbb{E}\|w_{\kappa}^{\ast}-\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}\|_{\mathbb{X}}^{m_{1}}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{3}.

Next, to tackle II, we know due to the well posedness and we have by the step before

{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}(\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}})=\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}.

To tackle III, due to the continuity of the operator

{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}
we know that there exists a function
\phi
with
\lim_{x\to 0}\phi(x)=0
, such that

\mathbb{E}\left\|{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}(\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}})-{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast})\right\|_{\mathbb{X}}^{m}\leq C\phi\left(\mathbb{E}\left\|\tilde{w}^{\ast}_{\kappa,\ell_{j},2^{\ell_{j}}}-w_{\kappa}^{\ast}\right\|_{\mathbb{X}}^{m_{1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{m}_{1}}

Finally, since
{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}=\mbox{Proj}_{\ell_{j}}{{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}}
and the convergence of the projection
\mbox{Proj}_{\ell_{j}}
, the difference

\mathbb{E}\left\|{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast})-{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa,\ell_{j}}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast})\right\|_{\mathbb{X}}^{m}

tends to zero (see remark
2.5
).
Finally, IV tends to zero, due to the continuity of the operator
{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}
.

As a consequence, we have

{\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast})=w_{\kappa}^{\ast},\quad{\mathbb{P}}^{\ast}\text{-a.s.}

As seen above,
w_{\kappa}^{\ast}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast}}(R_{\kappa})
,
so that by the assumption (
f
),

({\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast}),v^{1}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast}),\ldots,v^{J}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast}))\in C([0,T];U)\times C([0,T];U_{1})\cdot\cdot\cdot\times C([0,T];U_{J}),

Hence for
all
t\in[0,T]
,
{\mathbb{P}^{\ast}}
-a.s.

(w_{\kappa}^{\ast},v^{1}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast}),\ldots,v^{J}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast}))(t)=({\mathcal{V}}^{\kappa}_{\mathfrak{A}^{\ast},\mathbb{W}}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast}),v^{1}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast}),\ldots,v^{J}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast}))(t),

and by construction, we see that
(w_{\kappa}^{\ast},v^{1}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast}),\ldots,v^{J}(w_{\kappa}^{\ast}))
solves
the system (
2
)
on
{\mathfrak{A}}^{\ast}
and the proof is complete.

∎

Step (II)

Independent gluing together of the local solutions and Extension to a global solution:

In this step, we will show that
there exists a martingale solution of system (
2
).
That is, there exists a filtered probability space

\bar{\mathfrak{A}}:=(\bar{\Omega},\bar{\mathcal{F}},(\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0},\bar{\mathbb{P}})
,
a vector–valued Wiener process
\bar{\mathbb{W}}=(\bar{W}_{0},\bar{W}_{1},\ldots,\bar{W}_{J})
over
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}
,
and a tuple of processes
\bar{\mathfrak{w}}=(\bar{w},\bar{v}_{1},\ldots,\bar{v}_{J})
being a strong solution
of the system (
2
) over
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}
.

To do so, we construct a family of solutions
\{\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa}:\kappa\in\mathbb{N}\}
following the solution to the original problem until a certain stopping time
{\bar{\tau}}_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa})
(being defined later on in (
2.57
)). In particular, we will introduce for each
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
a new tuple of processes
\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa}
following the original system up to the stopping time
{\bar{\tau}}_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa})
. Besides, we will have

\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa}\big|_{\big[0,{\bar{\tau}}_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa})\big)}=\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa+1}\big|_{\big[0,{\bar{\tau}}_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa})\big)}.

Let us start with
\kappa=1
. From the steps before, we know there exists a martingale solution consisting of a probability space
\mathfrak{A}_{1}=(\Omega_{1},{{\mathcal{F}}}_{1},\mathbb{F}_{1},{\mathbb{P}}_{1})
, an independent vector valued Wiener process
\mathbb{W}_{1}=(W^{1}_{0},W^{1}_{1},\ldots,W^{1}_{J})
defined over
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
, and a process
\mathfrak{w}_{1}=(w_{1},v_{1}^{1},...,v_{1}^{J})
solving
{\mathbb{P}}_{1}
–a.s. the system

(2.30)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}dw_{1}(t)&=&\left(A_{0}w_{1}(t)+F_{0}(w_{1}(t),v^{1}_{1}(t),\cdots,v_{1}^{J}(t))\cdot\Xi^{0}_{1}(t)\,\right)\,dt+\Sigma_{0}(w_{1}(t))\,dW_{0}(t),\\
dv^{j}_{1}(t)&=&\left(A_{j}v^{j}_{1}(t)+F_{j}(w_{1}(t),v_{1}^{j}(t),v_{1}^{j+1}(t),\cdots,v_{1}^{J}(t))\cdot\Xi^{j}_{1}(t)\right)\,dt+\Sigma_{j}(v_{1}^{j}(t))\,dW_{j}(t),\\
&&\quad j=1,\ldots,J,\\
w(0)&=&w_{0},\\
v^{j}(0)&=&v^{j}_{0},\quad j=1,\ldots,J.\end{array}\right.

Let us define now the stopping times

\tau_{1}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{w}_{1}):=\inf\{s\geq 0\;\colon\;\max_{1\leq j\leq J}h_{j}(v^{j}_{1},s)\geq 1\}\wedge T,

on
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
.
Observe, on the time
interval
[0,\tau_{1}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{w}_{1})))
, the process
{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}
solves the original system given in
(
2
). Now, we define a new vector valued process
\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}
following
\mathfrak{w}_{1}
on
[0,\tau_{1}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{w}_{1}))
and extend this
process
to the whole interval
[0,T]
in the following way.
First, we put

\overline{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}:=\mathfrak{A}_{1}\ \text{ and }\ \bar{W}_{j}^{1}:={W}_{j}^{1},\ j=0,1,2,\ldots,J,

and let us introduce the processes
y_{1}
,
z^{1}_{1},\ldots,z^{J}_{1}
being a strong solution over
\overline{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}
to

(2.37)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}dy_{1}(t)&=&A_{0}y_{1}(t)+\Sigma_{0}(y_{1}(t))\,d(\theta_{\tau^{\ast}_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{1})}\bar{W}^{1}_{0})(t),\quad t\geq 0\\
dz^{j}_{1}(t)&=&A_{j}z^{j}_{1}(t)\,dt+\Sigma(z_{1}^{j}(t))\,d(\theta_{\tau^{\ast}_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{1})}\bar{W}^{1}_{j})(t),\quad j=1,\ldots,J,\quad t\geq 0,\\
y_{1}(0)&=&w(\tau^{\ast}_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{1})),\quad z^{j}_{1}(0)=v_{1}^{j}(\tau^{\ast}_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{1})),\quad j=1,\ldots,J.\end{array}\right.

Here,
\theta_{\sigma}
is the shift operator which maps
{\bar{W}}_{j}(t)
to
{\bar{W}}_{j}(t+\sigma)-\bar{W}_{j}(\sigma)
.
Following the idea of standard Theorems (see e.g.
[
1
, Theorem 2.5.1]
or Theorem 4.2.4
[
23
, p. 91]
) the existence of a unique solution
y_{1}
over
\overline{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}
to the first equation is obtained. Also following
[
28
, Theorem 6.12 ]
, the solutions to
(z^{1}_{1},z^{2}_{1},\ldots,z^{J}_{1})
are obtain by the fact that the operators
A_{j}
are linear.
Let us denote by
\mathfrak{y}_{1}
the tuple of processes
(y_{1},z_{1}^{1},\ldots,z_{1}^{J})
.
Now, let us define a process
\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}

being identical to
{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}
on the time interval
[0,\tau^{\ast}_{1}({\mathfrak{w}_{1}}))
and
following the linear part with noise, i.e.,

\mathfrak{y}_{1}(\tau^{\ast}_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{1})):=(y_{1}(\tau^{\ast}_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{1})),z_{1}^{1}(\tau^{\ast}_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{1})),\cdots,z_{1}^{J}(\mathfrak{w}_{1})),

afterwards.
In particular, let

\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}(t)=\begin{cases}{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}(t)&\mbox{ for }0\leq t<\tau^{\ast}_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{1}),\\
\mathfrak{y}_{1}(\tau^{\ast}_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{1}))&\mbox{ for }\tau^{\ast}_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{1})\leq t\leq T.\end{cases}

We also define

\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1})=\tau^{\ast}_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{1}).

Let us now construct the probability space and the processes for the next time interval. First, let
\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}(\tau^{\ast}_{1}(\mathfrak{w}_{1}))
have probability law
\mu_{1}
on
U_{0}\times U_{1}\times\cdots\times U_{J}
.

Again, from the first part, we know
there is a martingale solution consisting of a probability space
\mathfrak{A}_{2}=(\Omega_{2},{{\mathcal{F}}}_{2},\mathbb{F}_{2},{\mathbb{P}}_{2})
, an independent Wiener processes
\mathbb{W}_{2}=(W^{2}_{0},W^{2}_{1},\ldots,W^{2}_{J})
,
a process
\mathfrak{w}_{2}
solving
{\mathbb{P}}_{2}
-a.s. the system

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}dw_{2}(t)&=&\left(A_{0}w_{2}(t)+F_{0}(w_{2}(t),v^{1}_{2}(t),\cdots,v^{J}_{2}(t))\cdot\Xi^{0}_{2}(t)\,\right)\,dt+\Sigma_{0}(w_{2}(t))\,dW^{2}_{0}(t),\\
dv^{j}_{2}(t)&=&\left(A_{j}v^{j}_{2}(t)+F_{j}(w_{2}(t),v_{2}^{j}(t),v_{2}^{j+1}(t),\cdots,v_{2}^{J}(t))\cdot\Xi^{j}_{2}(t)\right)\,dt+\Sigma_{j}(v_{2}^{j}(t))\,dW^{2}_{j}(t),\end{array}\right.

with initial condition

\mathfrak{w}_{2}(0)=(w_{0}(\tau_{1}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{w}_{1}),v_{1}(\tau_{1}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{w}_{1})),\ldots,v_{1}(\tau_{1}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{w}_{1}))

having law
\mu_{1}
.
Let us define now the stopping times

\tau_{2}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{w}_{2}):=\inf\{s\geq 0\;\colon\;\max_{1\leq j\leq J}h_{j}(v^{j}_{2},s)\geq 2\}\wedge T,

on
\mathfrak{A}_{2}
.
Let
\overline{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}:=(\overline{\Omega}_{1},\overline{{{\mathcal{F}}}}_{1},\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{1},\overline{{\mathbb{P}}}_{1}):=\mathfrak{A}_{1}
, with
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{1}:=(\overline{{{\mathcal{F}}}}^{1}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
.
Let
\overline{\Omega}_{2}:=\overline{\Omega}_{1}\times\Omega_{2}
,
\overline{{{\mathcal{F}}}}_{2}:=\overline{{{\mathcal{F}}}}_{1}\otimes{{\mathcal{F}}}_{2}
,
\overline{{\mathbb{P}}}_{2}:=\overline{{\mathbb{P}}}_{1}\otimes{\mathbb{P}}_{2}
and let

\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}:=(\overline{{{\mathcal{F}}}}^{2}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
, where

\overline{{{\mathcal{F}}}}^{2}_{t}:=\begin{cases}\overline{{{\mathcal{F}}}}^{1}_{t},&\mbox{if}\quad t<\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}),\\
{{\mathcal{F}}}^{2}_{t-\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1})},&\mbox{if}\quad t\geq\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}).\end{cases}

Let
\overline{\mathfrak{A}}_{2}:=(\overline{\Omega}_{2},\overline{{{\mathcal{F}}}}_{2},\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{2},\overline{{\mathbb{P}}}_{2})
.
Finally, let us set for
j=0,1,2,\cdots J,

\bar{W}_{j}^{2}(t):=\begin{cases}\bar{W}_{j}^{1}(t),&\mbox{if}\quad t<\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}),\\
W_{j}^{2}({t-\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1})})+\bar{W}_{j}^{1}(\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1})),&\mbox{if}\quad t\geq\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}).\end{cases}

which gives a Wiener process w.r.t. the filtration
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
.

Now, let us define two processes
\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{2}

being identical to
\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}
on the time interval
[0,\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}))
, being identical to
\mathfrak{w}_{2}
on the time interval
[\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}),\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1})+\tau_{2}^{\ast}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{2}))
and
following the tuple of processes

\mathfrak{y}_{2}(t)=(y_{2}(t),z_{2}^{1}(t),\ldots,z_{2}^{J}(t)),\ t\geq 0,

solving the following system

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}dy_{2}(t)&=&A_{0}y_{2}(t)+\Sigma_{0}(y_{2}(t))\,d(\theta_{\bar{\tau}_{2}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{2})}\bar{W}^{2}_{0})(t),\quad t\geq 0\\
dz^{j}_{2}(t)&=&A_{j}z^{j}_{2}(t)\,dt+\Sigma_{j}(z_{2}^{j}(t))\,d(\theta_{\bar{\tau}_{2}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{2})}\bar{W}^{2}_{j})(t),\quad j=1,\ldots,J,\quad t\geq 0,\\
y_{2}(0)&=&w(\tau^{\ast}_{2}(\mathfrak{w}_{2})),\quad z^{j}_{2}(0)=v_{2}^{j}(\tau^{\ast}_{2}(\mathfrak{w}_{2})),\quad j=1,\ldots,J.\end{array}\right.

on
\overline{\mathfrak{A}}_{2}
with

\bar{\tau}_{2}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{2})=\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1})+\tau^{\ast}_{2}(\mathfrak{w}_{2}).

Let for
t\in[0,T]

afterwards.
In particular, we define

\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{2}(t)=\begin{cases}{\bar{\mathfrak{w}}}_{1}(t)&\mbox{ for }0\leq t<\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}),\\
{\mathfrak{w}}_{2}(t-\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}))&\mbox{ for }\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1})\leq t<\bar{\tau}_{2}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{2}),\\
\mathfrak{y}_{2}(\bar{\tau}_{2}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{2}))&\mbox{ for }\bar{\tau}_{2}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{2})\leq t\leq T.\end{cases}

By construction, we observe that

\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}\big|_{\big[0,{\bar{\tau}}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1})\big)}=\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{2}\big|_{\big[0,{\bar{\tau}}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1})\big)},

and
\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}
,
\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{2}
solve the original problem (
2
) until stopping times
{\bar{\tau}}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1})
and
{\bar{\tau}}_{2}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{2})
in the filtered probability space
\overline{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{A}}_{2}
respectively with respective associated Wiener processes
\bar{W}_{j}^{1}
and
\bar{W}_{j}^{2}
,
j=0,1,\cdots,J
.

In the same way we will construct for any
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
a
probability space
\mathfrak{A}_{\kappa}=(\Omega_{\kappa},{{\mathcal{F}}}_{\kappa},\mathbb{F}_{\kappa},{\mathbb{P}}_{\kappa})
, an independent Wiener processes
\mathbb{W}_{\kappa}=(W^{\kappa}_{0},W^{\kappa}_{1},\ldots,W^{\kappa}_{J})
,
a process
\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa}
solving
{\mathbb{P}}_{\kappa}
-a.s.

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}dw_{\kappa}(t)&=&\left(A_{0}w_{\kappa}(t)+F_{0}(w_{\kappa}(t),v^{1}(t)_{\kappa},\cdots,v^{J}_{\kappa}(t))\cdot\Xi^{0}_{\kappa}(t)\,\right)\,dt+\Sigma_{0}(w_{\kappa}(t))\,dW^{\kappa}_{0}(t),\\
dv^{j}_{\kappa}(t)&=&\left(A_{j}v^{j}_{\kappa}(t)+F_{j}(w_{\kappa}(t),v_{\kappa}^{j}(t),v_{\kappa}^{j+1}(t),\cdots,v_{\kappa}^{J}(t))\cdot\Xi^{j}_{\kappa}(t)\right)\,dt+\Sigma_{j}(v_{\kappa}^{j}(t))\,dW^{\kappa}_{j}(t),\end{array}\right.

with initial condition

\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa}(0)=(w_{0}(\tau_{\kappa-1}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa-1}),v_{\kappa-1}(\tau_{\kappa-1}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa-1})),\ldots,v_{\kappa-1}(\tau_{\kappa-1}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa-1}))

having law
\mu_{\kappa-1}
, with

\tau_{\kappa-1}^{\ast}(\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa-1}):=\inf\{s\geq 0\;\colon\;\max_{1\leq j\leq J}h_{j}(v^{j}_{\kappa-1},s)\geq\kappa-1\}\wedge T.

By induction, similarly, as done for
\kappa=2
we extend the filtered probability space and the Wienner processes as follows: We set

\overline{\Omega}_{\kappa}:=\overline{\Omega}_{\kappa-1}\times\Omega_{\kappa}
,
\overline{{{\mathcal{F}}}}_{\kappa}:=\overline{{{\mathcal{F}}}}_{\kappa-1}\otimes{{\mathcal{F}}}_{\kappa}
,
\overline{{\mathbb{P}}}_{\kappa}:=\overline{{\mathbb{P}}}_{\kappa-1}\otimes{\mathbb{P}}_{\kappa}
,

\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\kappa}:=(\overline{{{\mathcal{F}}}}^{\kappa}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]}
, where

\overline{{{\mathcal{F}}}}^{\kappa}_{t}:=\begin{cases}\overline{{{\mathcal{F}}}}^{\kappa-1}_{t},&\mbox{if}\quad t<\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa-1}),\\
{{\mathcal{F}}}^{\kappa}_{t-\bar{\tau}_{\kappa-1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa-1})},&\mbox{if}\quad t\geq\bar{\tau}_{\kappa-1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa-1}).\end{cases}

and let

\overline{\mathfrak{A}}_{\kappa}:=(\overline{\Omega}_{\kappa},\overline{{{\mathcal{F}}}}_{\kappa},\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\kappa},\overline{{\mathbb{P}}}_{\kappa})
and for
j=0,1,2,\cdots J,

\bar{W}_{j}^{\kappa}(t):=\begin{cases}\bar{W}_{j}^{\kappa-1}(t),&\mbox{if}\quad t<\bar{\tau}_{\kappa-1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa-1}),\\
W_{j}^{\kappa}({t-\bar{\tau}_{\kappa-1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa-1})})+\bar{W}_{j}^{\kappa-1}(\bar{\tau}_{\kappa-1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa-1})),&\mbox{if}\quad t\geq\bar{\tau}_{\kappa-1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa-1}).\end{cases}

which defines a Wiener process w.r.t. the filtration
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\kappa}
.

We also consider the following tuple of processes

\mathfrak{y}_{\kappa}(t)=(y_{\kappa}(t),z_{\kappa}^{1}(t),\ldots,z_{\kappa}^{J}(t)),\ t\geq 0,

solving the following system and following the linearized system

(2.52)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}dy_{\kappa}(t)&=&Ay_{\kappa}(t)+\Sigma_{0}(y_{\kappa}(t))\,d(\theta_{\bar{\tau}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa})}\bar{W}^{\kappa}_{0})(t),\quad t\geq 0\\
dz^{j}_{\kappa}(t)&=&A_{j}z^{j}_{\kappa}(t)\,dt+\Sigma(z_{\kappa}^{j}(t))\,d(\theta_{\bar{\tau}_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa})}\bar{W}^{\kappa}_{j})(t),\quad j=1,\ldots,J,\quad t\geq 0,\\
y_{\kappa}(0)&=&w(\tau^{\ast}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa})),\quad z^{j}_{\kappa}(0)=v_{\kappa}^{j}(\tau^{\ast}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa})),\quad j=1,\ldots,J.\end{array}\right.

afterwards on
\overline{\mathfrak{A}}_{\kappa}
where

\bar{\tau}_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa})=\bar{\tau}_{\kappa-1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa-1})+\tau^{\ast}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa}).

(2.57)

Finally, we construct

\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa}(t):=\begin{cases}{\bar{\mathfrak{w}}}_{\kappa-1}(t)&\mbox{ for }0\leq t<\bar{\tau}_{\kappa-1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa-1}),\\
{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa}(t-\bar{\tau}_{\kappa-1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa-1}))&\mbox{ for }\bar{\tau}_{\kappa-1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa-1})\leq t<\bar{\tau}_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa}),\\
\mathfrak{y}_{\kappa}(\bar{\tau}_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa}))&\mbox{ for }\bar{\tau}_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa})\leq t\leq T.\end{cases}

Besides, we know that

\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa-1}\big|_{\big[0,{\bar{\tau}}_{\kappa-1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa-1})\big)}=\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa}\big|_{\big[0,{\bar{\tau}}_{\kappa-1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa-1})\big)},

Note that, for any
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
, the processes
\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa}
solves system (
2
) on the time interval
[0,\bar{\tau}_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa})]
.
By the uniform bound, we will show that for all
T>0
with probability one we have

\lim\limits_{\kappa\to\infty}\bar{\tau}_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa})\geq T.

Now, due to assumption (
2.21
), we know that there exists a constant
C(T)>0
such that all cut-off functions are uniformly bounded
independent of
\kappa\geq 1
. Due to this property we will be able to extend our local solution to a global solution:
Let us define

\bar{\Omega}:=\prod_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}}\bar{\Omega}_{\kappa},\quad\bar{\mathcal{F}}:=\bigotimes_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}}\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{\kappa},\quad\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{t}:=\bigcap_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}}\bar{\mathcal{F}}^{\kappa}_{t},\ t\in[0,T],\quad\overline{\mathbb{F}}:=(\overline{{{\mathcal{F}}}}_{t})_{t\in[0,T]},\quad\bar{\mathbb{P}}:=\bigotimes_{\kappa\in\mathbb{N}}\bar{\mathbb{P}}_{\kappa},\quad\bar{\mathfrak{A}}=(\bar{\Omega},\bar{\mathcal{F}},\bar{{{\mathbb{F}}}},\bar{\mathbb{P}}),

\bar{\mathfrak{w}}(t):=(\bar{w},\bar{v}_{1},\bar{v}_{2},\cdots,\bar{v}_{J}):=\begin{cases}\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}(t),&\mbox{if}\quad 0\leq t<\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}),\\
\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa}(t),&\mbox{if}\quad\bar{\tau}_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa})\leq t<\bar{\tau}_{\kappa+1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa+1}),\quad\kappa\in\mathbb{N}.\end{cases}

and

\bar{W}_{j}(t):=\begin{cases}\bar{W}_{j}^{1}(t),&\mbox{if}\quad 0\leq t<\bar{\tau}_{1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{1}),\\
\bar{W}_{j}^{\kappa}(t),&\mbox{if}\quad\bar{\tau}_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa})\leq t<\bar{\tau}_{\kappa+1}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa+1}),\quad\kappa\in\mathbb{N}.\end{cases}\qquad\text{for }j=0,1,\cdots,J,

and observe that

(\bar{\mathbb{W}}(t):=(\bar{W}_{0}(t),\bar{W}_{1}(t),\cdots,\bar{W}_{J}(t)))_{t\in[0,T]},

is a Wiener processes with
respect to the filtration
\bar{{{\mathbb{F}}}}

over
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}
.

Next we will prove that the tuple
\left(\bar{\mathfrak{A}},\bar{\mathbb{W}},\bar{\mathfrak{w}}\right)
is in fact a global solution of the original system (
2
). For this aims, for any
\kappa\geq 1
, we define the set

\bar{\Omega}_{\kappa}:=\left\{\omega\in\bar{\Omega}\;\colon\;\bar{\tau}_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa})\geq T\right\}.

By construction, we know that the progressively measurable process
\bar{\mathfrak{w}}
, over
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}
solves the system (
2
) up to time
T
on the set
\bar{\Omega}_{\kappa}
. In particular, we have for the conditional probability

\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}\left(\{\omega\in\bar{\Omega}:\mbox{ $\bar{\mathfrak{w}}(\omega)$ solves systems \eqref{spdes1} up to times $T$}\}\mid\bar{\Omega}_{\kappa}\right)=1.

(2.58)

let us set

\bar{\Omega}_{0}=\bigcup_{\kappa=1}^{\infty}\bar{\Omega}_{\kappa}.

Then, since by construction we have
\bar{\Omega}_{\kappa}\subset\bar{\Omega}_{\kappa+1}
, we infer that

\displaystyle\qquad\bar{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left\{\mbox{$\omega\in\bar{\Omega}$: \mbox{ $\bar{\mathfrak{w}}(\omega)$ solves systems \eqref{spdes1} up to times $T$} }\right\}\cap\bar{\Omega}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\kappa\to\infty}\bar{\mathbb{P}}\left(\{\mbox{$\omega\in\bar{\Omega}$: \mbox{ $\bar{\mathfrak{w}}(\omega)$ solves systems \eqref{spdes1} up to times $T$} }\}\cap\bar{\Omega}_{\kappa}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{\kappa\to\infty}\bar{\mathbb{P}}\left(\{\mbox{$\omega\in\bar{\Omega}$: \mbox{ $\bar{\mathfrak{w}}(\omega)$ solves systems \eqref{spdes1} up to times $T$} }\}\mid\bar{\Omega}_{\kappa}\right)\cdot\bar{\mathbb{P}}\left(\bar{\Omega}_{\kappa}\right).

Due to (
2.58
)
it remains to show that

{\lim\limits_{\kappa\to\infty}}

\bar{{\mathbb{P}}}(\bar{\Omega}_{\kappa})=1
.
However, we note that by construction of the positive stopping times
\bar{\tau}_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa})
in (
2.57
), for any
\kappa\geq 1
, the following injection holds

\left\{\omega\in\bar{\Omega}:\bar{\tau}_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa})<T\right\}\subset\left\{\omega\in\bar{\Omega}:\tau^{\ast}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa})<T\right\},

and therefore by definition of stopping times
\tau^{\ast}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa})
, for any
\kappa\geq 1
, we derive that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left\{\omega\in\bar{\Omega}\setminus\bar{\Omega}_{\kappa}\right\}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}\left\{\omega\in\bar{\Omega}:\bar{\tau}_{\kappa}(\bar{\mathfrak{w}}_{\kappa})<T\right\}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left\{\omega\in\bar{\Omega}:\tau^{\ast}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa})<T\right\}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left\{\omega\in\bar{\Omega}:h_{j}(v^{j}_{\kappa},\tau^{\ast}_{\kappa}(\mathfrak{w}_{\kappa}))\geq\kappa\right\}.

From assumption (
2.21
) we know there exists a (
\kappa
-independent) constant
C(T)>0
and
{m_{j}}>0
,
j=1,2,\cdots,J
such that we have

\max_{j=1,\ldots,J}\bar{\mathbb{E}}\left[h^{m_{j}}_{j}(v^{j}_{\kappa},t)\right]\leq C(T),\quad t\in[0,T],\quad\kappa\in\mathbb{N}.

Thus we have by the Markov inequality

{\mathbb{P}}\left\{\omega\in\bar{\Omega}\setminus\bar{\Omega}_{\kappa}\right\}\leq\frac{C(T)}{\kappa^{m_{j}}}\to 0,\qquad\mbox{ as }\kappa\to\infty.

Hence the solution process is well defined on
\bar{\Omega}_{0}=\bigcup_{\kappa=1}^{\infty}\bar{\Omega}_{\kappa}
with
{\mathbb{P}}(\bar{\Omega}_{0})=1
.
This finishes the proof.
\square

∎

3.
Application to stochastic chemotaxis on fluid model

Chemotaxis is defined as the oriented movement of cells (or an organism) in response to a chemical gradient. Many different kinds of motile cells undergo chemotaxis. For example, bacteria and many
amoeboid cells move toward a food source; immune cells like macrophages and neutrophils move towards invading cells in our bodies. Other cells, connected with the immune response and wound healing, are attracted to areas of inflammation by chemical signals.
In particular, there are many processes in nature governed by chemotaxis. Besides Pfeffer, who described already chemotaxis
1884
by sperm offern and mosses
[
27
]
,
Raper studied in
1935
chemotaxis at the cellular slime mold
Dictyostelium discoideuum
, a simple model of morphogenesis
[
29
]
.
During its life cycle, a population of ‘Dicto’ grows by cell division as long as there is sufficient nourishment. If food is rare and the environment living
conditions are getting poor; then the individual amoeba starts to emit CAM
(Cyclic Adenosine Monophosphate).
